## Page 1


Mad Props: Parallelism in Markov Chain Monte Carlo Through the
Lens of the Infinite Proposal Limit
Nathan E. Glatt-Holtz♥, Andrew J. Holbrook♦, Justin A. Krometis♣, Cecilia F. Mondaini♠
♥Department of Statistics and Department of Mathematics, Indiana University, Bloomington
♦Department of Biostatistics, University of California, Los Angeles
♣National Security Institute and Department of Mathematics, Virginia Polytechnic Institute and State University
♠Department of Mathematics, Drexel University, Philadelphia, PA
emails: negh@iu.edu, aholbroo@g.ucla.edu, jkrometi@vt.edu, cf823@drexel.edu
May 22, 2026
Abstract
Multiproposal MCMC (MP-MCMC) algorithms use clouds of proposals to efficiently traverse state
spaces and overcome complex target geometries. While MCMC methods are embarrassingly parallel by
nature, the non-trivial forms of parallelism provided by the MP-MCMC formalism sometimes leads to
significant improvements over a naive approach. Here, one important tuning parameter is the number
of proposals p used by a single MP-MCMC iteration. While a number of computational strategies have
been proposed to efficiently leverage large numbers of proposals within the MP-MCMC paradigm, much
remains unknown about these algorithms, particularly in the large p-regime.
In this contribution, we discover surprising results by identifying and studying several promising
new methods (Algorithm 1.1, Algorithm 3.3, Algorithm 3.4), ruling out other extant approaches and
discovering new relationships between different MP-MCMC methodologies. Our analysis is centered on
a general state space multiproposal involutive theory recently constructed by the authors [GHHKM24,
GHHK+24] combined with the consideration of the large p-limit kernels for MP-MCMC algorithms within
a variety of different classes of proposal and acceptance structures.

Introduction
Generating random samples from complex or high-dimensional probability distributions remains a central
challenge in 21st century science. Among the most successful strategies is the class of classical (Metropolis-
type) Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) algorithms dating back to the late 1940’s [MRR+53, Has70].
These methods effectively resolve simple, low-dimensional target distributions by generating a single proposal
state and then randomly accepting or rejecting this proposal with a prescribed probability. Since that time,
a wide variety of more sophisticated MCMC algorithms have been advanced. This growing body of Monte
Carlo methods, combined with an ever advancing technological substrate, has allowed for the resolution
of increasingly sophisticated higher-dimensional statistical models. Nevertheless, there is always a need to
address ever more challenging models, and the frontier for MCMC algorithm design and its foundational
theory remains open in many directions.
One important class of methods at this frontier are the so called multiproposal MCMC (MP-MCMC)
algorithms [LLW00, Tje04, Fre04, DJ09, Nea11, Cal14, LT19, Hol23a, GHHKM24, LCL+25, GHHK+24,
SGHC+26] that use a ‘cloud’ of proposals at each MCMC iteration to explore a target distribution more
efficiently. While, in general, MCMC methods are embarrassingly parallel by nature, the non-trivial forms
of parallelism that arise within the MP-MCMC formalism can lead to significant improvements over a
naive approach.
So far, this MP-MCMC approach is proving especially beneficial for sampling targets
with multimodal and nonlinear geometries by significantly improving mixing rates while avoiding costly or
infeasible gradient computations, [Hol23b, BGHK23, GHHKM24, LCL+25, GHHK+24, SGHC+26]. On the
other hand, applying the MP-MCMC formalism in practice requires navigating a landscape of seemingly
distinct algorithmic structures (e.g.
multiple-try Metropolis, convolutional proposal structures, local vs
global methods, HMC-type approaches...) and then specifying a number of tuning parameters the exact
choices of which may depend on target geometry and the available computational resources. Thus, before
a scientist does the work of crafting an MP-MCMC algorithm and programming GPU code to efficiently
scale this algorithm to use a large number of proposals, they might ask: is it worth the effort? And, since
MP-MCMC comprises a large class of algorithms, to what extent do the benefits of using large numbers of
proposals depend on algorithmic specifics?
With this backdrop in mind, this contribution provides a unified analysis of several large classes of
MP-MCMC Markov processes.
Our systematic approach leverages the dual-lenses of the large proposal
p →∞limit, presaged in [LLW00, GMZ23], alongside a general state space involutive theory for reversible
Metropolis-type kernels recently advanced in a series of works [GHKM23, NWEV20, ALL20, GHHKM24,
GHHK+24]. This dual approach clarifies delicate relationships between a number of existing methods in this
complex algorithm landscape while, along the way, largely ruling out certain extant approaches. Furthermore
we discover some promising new MP-MCMC methods including the “slingshot” Algorithm 1.1 as well as
two “Hilbert space” or “Preconditioned” methods in Algorithm 3.3, Algorithm 3.4. We refer to the first
algorithm as the “slingshot” because its acceptance structure includes a bias toward accepting proposal points
far from the current state. As we unfold below, this curious kernel has a number of interesting and desirable
properties. In particular it leaves its target invariant in the large p-limit despite failing to do so for finite
p while providing significant degrees of freedom in parameter auto-tuning over other methods. Regarding
Algorithm 3.3 and Algorithm 3.4, we provide two new ‘dimension-free’ methods built on a preconditioned
Crank-Nicolson (pCN)-type proposal structure [Nea98, BRSV08, CRSW13] in the multiple-try-metropolis
(MTM) vein [LLW00]. These two new methods focus on the global and local structure of the target measure,
respectively. Finally it is notable that our analysis provides some interesting technical improvements to both
the large p and involutive methodologies. For the former we note Theorem 4.3 which leverages the uniform law
of Large Numbers (cf. [Tal87, DGZ91, vH14, Wai19]) to develop this p →∞limit for multiple-try Metropolis
kernels on general state spaces. For the latter, we expand the scope of the main result [GHHKM24, Theorem
2.2] as Theorem 2.3, which provides a formulation allowing us to establish Theorem 3.8, again pertinent to
MTM type kernels on general state spaces.
Overview of Main Results
Let us turn to provide a more systematic overview of our contributions herein alongside some further com-
mentary on related developments in the existing literature. Our aim is to address the MP-MCMC paradigm

BGHK20a, BGHK23] and in sampling conditioned diffusions [BRSV08, CRSW13]. Note that the Hilbert
space approach was recently generalized to our MP-MCMC setting in [GHHKM24]. We derive two further
new MP-MCMC methods suited for (1.3) in this vein herein as Algorithm 3.3, Algorithm 3.4.

We emphasize that, without further conditions, (1.4) is only an illuminating way of organizing our
analysis and a general structure which we use to compute the limit as p →∞for a number of special cases in
Section 4, Section 5. Indeed, at the given degree of generality, (1.4) has no relation to our target µ. Here, the
involutive theory of [GHHKM24], recalled and slightly generalized below as Theorem 2.3, gives a powerful,
concise and broadly applicable criterion for the reversibility of (1.4) with respect to a given µ as a function
of the elements V, Sj, αj. We make significant further use of this criteria herein noting in particular an
application that broadens the scope of multiple-try metropolis (MTM) MP-MCMC methods in Theorem 3.8
and yielding in particular the new methods Algorithm 3.3 and Algorithm 3.4. Also we observe that the
formulation (1.4) exposes degeneracies that we use to essentially rule out ‘Metropolis-type’ multiproposal
structures as we unfold below in Theorem 2.1, Theorem 2.4 and Theorem 3.5.
Of course we must keep in mind the computational complexity and implementation details as we pro-
ceed. Here we observe that certain naive unbiased proposal schemes under Theorem 2.3 result in acceptance
mechanisms that require burdensome O(p2) floating-point operations which are not amenable to straight-
forward parallelization (see (3.6) for example). On the other hand, various methods developed in previous

herein.

5
Rao-Blackwellization is an importance sampler for µ (see (3.17)).
Secondly we show that the p = ∞
limiting process is simply independent samples from the target µ (Theorem 4.2, Section 4.2.3). We carry out
empirical studies of Algorithm 1.1 in Section 6, providing some initial empirical evidence of the promise of
Algorithm 1.1. Here a state-dependent, total variation norm minimizing, proposal standard deviation σf(q)
derived in Section 6.1.1 improves mixing and the rate of convergence of the associated kernels to the p = ∞
limit. Interestingly, this strongly state-specific scaling violates popular adaptive tuning criteria, such as the
diminishing adaptations criterion [RR09], established for unbiased kernels, and neither Algorithm 1.1 nor
Theorem 3.1 yield generally unbiased Markov kernels that leave the target invariant.
In summary, while each of the above described acceptance structures from (1.5) have their advantages,
none of them are generically unbiased.
Indeed, as we compute below, to have an unbiased acceptance
structure directly from (1.5), we obtain an unwieldy and computationally expensive acceptance structure
(see (3.6)), which requires O(p2) floating point operations and which is not easily amenable to efficient
parallelization. Thus, the first significance of the large proposal limit (Theorem 4.1, Theorem 4.2) has been
made plain as we introduced the various methods above, not least Algorithm 1.1: it allows us to identify
algorithms that, while biased at the finite p, are asymptotically unbiased.
Of course it may be computationally infeasible to rely on a p →∞limit. In previous literature, two
approaches have been proposed to sidestep the inherent bias in (1.5) while avoiding the expensive acceptance
structure given by (3.6). In one direction the Multiple Try Metropolis (MTM) [LLW00] removes bias using a
two-step acceptance strategy. Meanwhile the convolutional proposal approach developed in [Tje04, Hol23a,
GHHKM24] does so with a two-step proposal strategy instead. Here our involutive perspective allows us to
broaden the scope of both approaches. Regarding the MTM approach our general involutive framework allows
us to consider this class of methods in a general state space setting. Our result here, Theorem 3.8, includes
the Hilbert space (or preconditioned) methods suited for the setting (1.3). Thus, Theorem 3.8 provides an
interesting complement/counterpoint to the mpCN methods we developed recently in [GHHKM24, BGHK23,
GHHK+24]. We summarize the new MTM-pCN methods that fall out of Theorem 3.8 as Theorem 3.9,
Algorithm 3.3 and as Theorem 3.11, Algorithm 3.4. Furthermore, we make clear in Section 3.2 that the
slingshot, local, and bubble bath methods can all be ‘corrected for bias’ by introducing an appropriate
second accept-reject step (see Theorem 3.8).
Note that while the MTM strategy’s use of p −1 auxiliary variables z(k+1)
1
, . . . , z(k+1)
p−1
nearly doubles
the amount of computation required compared to, e.g., the convolutional proposal approach described in
Section 3.3, the latter approach is not without issue. Theorem 4.17 shows that, for a single MCMC iteration,
convolutional proposal approaches fail when one tries to incorporate all proposals within a single estimator,
as the variance of such Rao-Blackwellized estimators does not converge to 0 in the large p-limit. A seeming
contradiction arises: convolutional proposal algorithms leave their target invariant but their iteration-wise
Rao-Blackwellizations’ mean squared errors do not converge to 0 in the large p-limit, while the slingshot
(Algorithm 1.1) fails to leave its target invariant (for finite p) but furnishes kernels with asymptotically
exact Rao-Blackwellizations. In any event, the relative performance of the MTM vs convolutional approaches
constitutes a delicate open question which is an interesting topic for future research as we lay out in Section 7.
In addition to shedding light on algorithms that use and avoid (1.5), we discover two degeneracies in the
involutive theory that shut the door on two needlessly computationally expensive MP-MCMC approaches.
The first degeneracy holds at a high level of abstraction (see Theorem 2.1, Theorem 2.5), making plain
that various methods with a Metropolis-type (2.22) rather than a Barker-type (2.21) acceptance mechanism
reduce to simple mixture of single-proposal methods. From a theoretical perspective, the latter distinction
is particularly pertinent, as the famous Peskun-optimality [Pes73, Tie98] of the classical MH kernel does
not apply to the multiproposal setting, particularly in the large p-limit. The practical takeaway is that
computationally intensive MP-MCMC algorithms that use the Metropolis-type acceptance are exactly as
useful as their single-proposal predecessor. Indeed, this result may accord with intuition. The Metropolis-
type acceptance probabilities of (2.22) have an apparent drawback: the function min(1, ·) removes the
competitive advantage of proposals with larger target probabilities among those proposals for which target
probabilities exceed unity. Meanwhile, the Barker-type acceptances feature no such leveling device.
Section 5 contains a second degeneracy, which holds for MP-MCMC algorithms that use intermediate
numerical integration points in an HMC-type scheme as proposals. Theorem 5.2 establishes that, as long as
the numerical integration technique is consistent in the small step size-limit, multiple multiproposal HMC
kernels degenerate to vanilla HMC with random integration times.
From a theoretical perspective, the


## Page 6


Mad Props: Parallelism in MCMC Through the Lens of the Infinite Proposal Limit
6
result is interesting insofar as it holds for both Metropolis- and Barker-type acceptances, the former being a
fortiori degenerate regardless of proposal count, the latter achieving degeneracy only within the large p-limit
of the multiproposal HMC context. The practical upshot is, again, that certain computationally intensive
MP-MCMC algorithms are exactly as useful as an easy-to-program, computationally inexpensive, traditional
HMC algorithm with small modification.
Finally let us make a few further remarks on the passage to the p →∞limit which we carry out in
Section 4 and in Section 5. Rigorously establishing this limit in the weak sense is relatively straightforward
mathematically for (1.5), its convolutional variant (3.44) in Theorem 4.1, and for the (degenerate) MP-
MCMC HMC structures in Theorem 5.2. On the other hand, the MTM p →∞is more challenging due to
non-trivial correlations in the kernels which appear in this case (see (3.25)). As described in Theorem 4.3, this
limit yields a traditional Metropolis-Hastings kernel built from a proposal given by the p →∞limit of the
proposal step as defined in Theorem 4.2. While analogous results had previously been considered in [GMZ23]
for some continuous targets on Rd, we are able to treat the general state space case here. Our approach uses
a delicate compactness argument leveraging the uniform law of large numbers, [Tal87, DGZ91, vH14, Wai19]
i.e. by identifying an appropriate Glivenko-Cantelli class in the interaction between the weighted acceptance
structure β and the cloud proposal kernel Q appearing in (3.25).
The rest of the manuscript is organized as follows: Section 2 sets out the general framework around (1.1),
(1.4) which culminates in our extension of the involutive framework in Theorem 2.3. Then in Section 3 we
comprehensively lay out various methods including all of our novel algorithmic contributions arising from
(1.5) and its Multiple Try and Convolution variations. In Section 4 we carry out all the p →∞limits for
a broad class of kernels which include all the methods in Section 3 as special cases. In this section we also
use the derived limit kernels to complete the picture sketched previously in Section 3. Section 5 is devoted
to establishing the degeneracy of some HMC-type multiproposal methods. Meanwhile, Section 6 gives some
extended case study for Algorithm 1.1. Finally Section 7 provides an outlook and considers some possible
avenues for future research suggested by the results herein. While mathematically precise statements and
sketches of proof are provided in the main body of the manuscript, rigorous proofs are deferred for Section A.

Preliminaries: The general state space and involutive frame-
works
Following our recent works [GHHKM24, GHHK+24], we begin by introducing a flexible, general state-space
formalism which provides the setting and notational conventions for all of the multiproposal algorithms we
study in what follows. In particular, this framework encompasses a class of ‘convolutional’ multiproposal
kernels considered previously in [Tje04, GHHKM24, GHHK+24], the two stage Multiple Try (MT) methods
from [LT19, LLW00] as well as some new algorithms with a similar proposal structure which we introduce
here. On the other hand, our abstract set-up also admits multiproposal versions of the HMC algorithm. In
particular this encompasses the situation where we proceed at each step by selecting among different points
on the (numerically integrated) Hamiltonian path as we consider below in Section 5.
We proceed as follows: assume (X, ΣX), (Y, ΣY ) are measurable spaces, and let V : X × ΣY →[0, 1]
be a Markov kernel. Namely, we assume V(q0, ·) is a probability measure for each fixed q0 ∈X and that
q0 7→V(q0, A) is a measurable map over all A ∈ΣY . Now, for a given number p ≥1 of proposals, we consider
also a collection of p + 1 measurable mappings on X × Y , S = (S0, . . . , Sp) and we let α = (α0, . . . , αp)
be a collection of acceptance probabilities. Thus, for j = 0, . . . , p, we suppose that αj : X × Y →[0, 1] is

Remark 2.1. At this level of generality, the kernel P defined by (1.4) (and its implementation (2.4)) is just
a convenient overarching representation encompassing any multiproposal MCMC algorithm. In fact, as we
previously observed in [GHHKM24], this kernel includes any Markov transition semigroup R on a measurable
space (X, ΣX) by taking Y = X, p = 0, V(q, dv) = R(q, dv), α0 = 1, and finally S0(q, v) = (v, q). It is also
worth noting that one algorithm (or equivalently Markov transition kernel) can have multiple (non-trivial)
representations of the form (1.4). This is a point we previously explored in depth in [GHKM23, Section 3].
Here we take the opportunity to point out a particular degeneracy in (1.4) which we will return to several

2.1
Criteria for Reversibility
As we observed in [GHHK+24] and recalled here in Theorem 2.2, the Markov transition kernels defined by
(1.4) are extremely general, covering in fact all Markov kernels as a special case without imposing further
restrictions. Thus, we need criteria to identify interesting subclasses in which to consider the p →∞limit in
(1.4). One obvious criterion for determining the elements (V, S, α) defining P is a guarantee of unbiasedness
of P with respect to the given target measure µ on (X, ΣX). Note at the outset however that unbiasedness
is not an absolute requirement as we discuss extensively below (see Theorem 2.5).
In [GHHKM24], we developed a unified ‘involutive’ theory providing a criteria for reversibility (and
thus unbiasedness) of multiproposal algorithms relative to a given target measure µ on a measurable space
(X, ΣX). This work extends earlier results in [GHKM23, ALL20, NWEV20] from the single step MP-MCMC
setting. We next recall this theory providing a few new refinements here which will be used in what follows.
In order to proceed we recall a few definitions. Given measurable spaces (Z, ΣZ), (Z′, ΣZ′), a probability
measure N on (Z, ΣZ), and a measurable function F : Z →Z′, the pushforward of N under F, denoted
as F ∗N, is the probability measure on (Z′, ΣZ′) defined as F ∗N(A) = N(F −1(A)) for any A ∈ΣZ′.
In other words, if z ∼N then F(z) ∼F ∗N. Next, if N, ˜

Remark 2.5. We should emphasize an unbiasedness requirement is not absolute and will not always be

In any event, as we proceed, other motivations for the form of P (V,S,α) in (1.4) will appear suggesting the
salience of some biased methods. This includes the structure of the associated Rao-Blackwellized estimators
(2.10) and the implied p →∞kernel and its unbiasedness; see Theorem 4.2 and Section 4.2.3 below. We
will also consider situations which up to the present merely arise as immediate simplifications starting from
an unbiased kernel featuring a cheaper to evaluate structure as in Theorem 3.1. In some cases these kernels
yield desirable empirical properties suggesting rigorously unjustified methods worthy of further consideration
in the future.
3
A selection of Multiproposal Algorithms
In this section, we describe a collection of multiproposal methods built on a proposal structure based on
i.i.d. samples from a fixed single proposal kernel, conditional on the current state; see (1.5). Thus we are
interested here in various multiproposal generalizations of the random walk Metropolis-Hastings (RWMH).
In Section 3.1, we consider several different acceptance mechanisms associated with this proposal structure.
In particular, we detail a new (biased) method which we refer to as ‘the Slingshot’ previewed above in the
introduction as Algorithm 1.1.
Since the methods we introduce in Section 3.1 are biased in general we then describe in Section 3.2,
Section 3.3 two different strategies to obtain unbiased methods by modifying slightly the proposal structure
or acceptance mechanisms. First, in Section 3.2, we consider extensions of the Multiple Try (MT) approach
pioneered in [LLW00], which is based on a two stage acceptance structure. By leveraging Theorem 2.3, we
generalize this Multiple Try approach yielding an unbiased correction of Algorithm 1.1. We also introduce
two new Hilbert space methods in the vein of the pCN algorithm, [BRSV08, CRSW13], as Algorithm 3.3,
Algorithm 3.4. In contrast, in Section 3.3, we review some ‘convolutional’ methods which add a preliminary


## Page 11


Mad Props: Parallelism in MCMC Through the Lens of the Infinite Proposal Limit
11
step to the proposal mechanism, (3.44), to provide a different means of obtaining an unbiased algorithm,
including [Tje04], and a third Multiproposal pCN which we recently derived in [GHHKM24, GHHK+24].
Recall that our main interest herein is to analyze the large proposal limit of certain relevant subclasses of
multiproposal algorithms falling under the framework of (1.4). As such, the particular methods outlined will
frame the general setting of Section 4 below, where our rigorous results will underline the salience of some
methods, most prominently the slingshot, Algorithm 1.1, while highlighting some possible disadvantages of
others. Note also that, further on in Section 5, we take up (and then largely rule out the salience of) some
multiproposal versions of the Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) approach where our proposals consist of
different time steps along the approximated Hamiltonian dynamic.
3.1
Independent Proposal Structures
Let us begin with some methods around the proposal structures of the form (1.5). To formalize this in
our abstract setup, (1.4), consider a (single proposal) Markov transition kernel Q : X × ΣX →[0, 1] on a
measurable space (X, ΣX) where the target probability measure µ of interest is defined. To complete the
formal description of our proposal structure defined by (V, S), we take Y = Xp (with the usual product
σ-algebra) and set S to be the coordinate-flip involutions, namely
Sj(q0, . . . , qp) := (qj, q1, . . . , qj−1, q0, qj+1, . . . , qp),
(q0, . . . , qp) ∈Xp+1, j = 0, . . . , p.

In this ‘Hilbert space’ setting, the formulation (3.3) is unavailable for (3.5). See e.g. [CRSW13, GHKM23]
for further context on this latter class of proposal kernels.

Method 3.2 (‘Local’ proposals). When generating a proposal cloud via (3.2), an ‘aggressive’ (e.g. high
variance) Q can be understood as a ‘global’ search for high probability regions of the target measure. By
contrast, a ‘conservative’ Q may be interpreted as exploring the local structure of the target measure around
the current state. In this latter conservative setting, different acceptance probabilities can be used to achieve
an (over-damped) Langevin type method in the large p and small single-proposal variance σ2 limit.
To make this explicit, consider the case of a continuous target, µ(d˜q) = π(˜q)d˜q, and a Gaussian single
proposal kernel of the form Qσ(q0, d˜q) = N(q0, σ2I)(d˜q). That is, Q in (3.2) is of the form (3.4) with

Method 3.4 (The Slingshot Algorithm). Another new class of algorithms arising out of the proposal struc-
ture (3.1), (3.2) has a general tendency to accept proposals that are far from the current point, leading to
our moniker ‘the Slingshot’. Although biased in general, this (class of) methods has many interesting and
desirable properties. In addition to the theoretical results in Section 4 (see in particular Section 4.2.3), we
carry out some numerical case studies of this method below in Section 6.

Finally, we observe that, while the slingshot is in general biased for fixed p, its limit as p →∞is
asymptotically unbiased. Indeed, this limit kernel is just µ itself, so that we are simply considering Monte
Carlo samples from µ in this limit. See Theorem 4.2 and Section 4.2.3 below.

3.2
The Multiple-Try (MT) Approach
We turn next to provide our general state space formulation of the Multiple Try (MT) approach. As noted in
the introduction, this family of (unbiased) algorithms has been previously introduced in [LLW00] and studied
in a number of subsequent works [CL07, BDM12, CLL+22, YL23, LT19]. Expanding on this paradigm, we
now show that each of the (biased) algorithms laid out in Theorem 3.1, Theorem 3.2, Theorem 3.3 and
Theorem 3.4 (essentially) provide different preliminary proposal steps for different (unbiased) Multiple Try
algorithms.

The following result establishing the unbiasedness of (3.25), namely Algorithm 3.2, includes [LLW00] as
a special case.
Theorem 3.8. Fix a target probability measure µ on (X, ΣX). Consider a measurable function β : X ×X →
(0, ∞), a Markov transition kernel Q : X × ΣX →[0, 1], and number of proposals p ≥1. Suppose that η and
η⊥defined in (3.24) satisfy η⊥≪η. Then P defined according to (3.25) is reversible with respect to µ.
The proof of Theorem 3.8 is based on Theorem 2.3, and is provided below in Section A.2.
We now turn to provide the Multiple Try correction to each of the methods defined previously in Sec-

Method 3.13 (Tjelmeland, Multiproposal pCN). Specializing mostly to the case Q = ¯Q in (3.44), where
(3.47) reduces to (3.32), and maintaining S as in (3.1), we obtain several concrete algorithms which have
been previously considered. Indeed, under these circumstances, when X = Rd and we are aiming to resolve
a continuously distributed target µ, that is where µ0 is the Lebesgue measure in (1.1), then (3.32) holds for
any symmetric kernel as in (3.34). This is the method discovered in [Tje04]. Note that the more flexible
condition (3.47) allows for the consideration of some non-symmetric proposal structures; see [GHHKM24,
Algorithm 4].
In the infinite-dimensional setting (1.3), taking Q = ¯Q as in (3.5) leads to a multiproposal version of the
pCN algorithm from [Nea98, BRSV08, CRSW13], which we derived in [GHHKM24], [GHHK+24]. Here π

## Page 21


Mad Props: Parallelism in MCMC Through the Lens of the Infinite Proposal Limit
21
In [SC21] this resampling strategy is advocated as an approximation to the Rao-Blackwellization as in
(2.10) for various multiproposal methods. Such a resampling approach may thus provide some of the advan-
tages of Rao-Blackwellization but with less arduous memory requirements. As we discuss in Section 7, this
is an interesting direction for future research around the various methods introduced in this section.
4
Large proposal limit for conditionally independent proposals
In this section, we analyze the large proposal limit for a general class of conditionally independent Barker-
type multiproposal algorithms motivated by and encompassing each of the different methods introduced
across Section 3. Specifically, we consider this p →∞limit for kernels of the form (3.51) or (3.25), where
we recall Table 1, which summarizes how we recover each of the concrete methods in Section 3 with an
appropriate choice of β : X × X →(0, ∞) and the single proposal kernels Q, ¯Q.
Our analysis is divided into five subsections. Section 4.1 deals with the weak limit as p →∞for both
(3.51) and (3.25), given in Theorem 4.1 and Theorem 4.3, respectively. Theorem 4.3 lays out a novel ap-
proach to the p →∞limit for the Multiple Try methods introduced in Section 3.2. Next, in Section 4.2, we
explain how Theorem 4.1 and Theorem 4.3 apply to each of the methods summarized in Table 1. In par-
ticular, we justify the asymptotic unbiasedness of Algorithm 1.1 on the basis of Theorem 4.2 (Section 4.2.3,
Theorem 4.10). Here, we also carefully address the infinite dimensional scope of Algorithm 3.3 and Algo-
rithm 3.4 (Theorem 4.5, Theorem 4.8). In Section 4.3, we then consider various algorithmic parameter limits
at p = ∞from the kernels derived in Section 4.1. This analysis further motivates and classifies the ‘global’
(Theorem 3.1) and ‘local’ (Theorem 3.2, Theorem 3.3) approaches as well as their unbiased counterparts
provided in Section 3.2, Section 3.3. Section 4.4 provides quantitative convergence results under more restric-
tive conditions on β in total variation distance for (3.51). In particular, the main result in this subsection
(Theorem 4.15) plays a central role in our tuning analysis for the Slingshot algorithm below in Section 6.
Finally, in Section 4.5, we estimate the variance of the one step Rao-Blackwellization associated to (3.51).
Here one notable consequence is that this variance vanishes in the case where there is no preliminary proposal
as in Theorem 3.1 and Theorem 3.4. This suggests an advantage of these methods over Theorem 3.13.
4.1

structure.

The boundedness condition in (4.26) is easy to understand and to verify in many situations of interest. On
the other hand, (4.24), (4.25) or (4.27), (4.28) can be seen to hold when π or Φ, respectively, are continuously
differentiable with their derivatives growing at no more than a small quadratic exponential rate at ‘spatial
infinity’; see (4.29). In particular, the conditions imposed in the second case in Theorem 4.5 include in its
scope some interesting targets arising in the Bayesian approach to PDE inverse problems. We make these
observations precise in Theorem 4.6 immediately below.
Having surmounted the challenge of identifying a set of tractable conditions on the target, the task of
verifying the conditions in Theorem 4.3 for the proof of Theorem 4.5 is otherwise mostly routine. The one
subtle point is establishing (H4) in case (ii), which involves the use of compactness in the infinite dimensional
Gaussian measure theory setting. Complete details are provided below in Section A.5.

The scope of applicability of (ii) in Theorem 4.5 includes a variety of Bayesian PDE inverse problems of
the type developed in e.g. [Stu10, BGHK20a]. Here in principle we could formulate general conditions of the
type (4.29), but the functional-analytic considerations are much more delicate and vary greatly in specifics
from problem to problem. Thus, it is more useful and illuminating to illustrate how things play out for specific
model problems. Here, we treat a problem concerning the Bayesian estimation of a velocity field from the
concentration of a solute sitting in this flow, as we developed previously in [BGHK20a, BGHK20b, GHM21,
GHHKM24].
For this model problem, our target is of the type (1.1)–(1.3) where the potential takes the form
Φ(q) = |Γ−1/2(Y −O(θ(q)))|2.
(4.32)

4.3
Algorithmic Parameter Limits at p = ∞: Local vs Global
Having now computed the p →∞limits for some concrete methods in Section 4.2 based on Theorem 4.1
and Theorem 4.3, we now turn to some algorithmic parameter limits at p = ∞. Specifically, we consider
certain limits for the parameters σ in (3.4) and ρ in (3.5), treating the ‘global’ and ‘local’ methods from
Section 3 successively. In the former ‘global’ situation, we establish the limiting cases σ →∞or ρ →0
in the proposal mechanism, toward i.i.d. Monte Carlo draws from the target measure in Theorem 4.11.
Meanwhile, Theorem 4.14 shows that the ‘local’ methods converge towards appropriate Langevin dynamics
in the opposite extreme, that is either σ →0 or ρ →1 in the proposal structure.
4.3.1

Theorem 3.9 and Theorem 3.13 to the kernel µ(d˜q) for i.i.d. samples from the target measure in the limit
as σ →∞.

Remark 4.12 (Large p Algorithmic Parameter Scaling: ‘Global’ Proposal Structures). Our claims about ρ
and σ in (4.62), (4.65) are made here at p = ∞. The optimal relationship between ρ and p, or between σ
and p is a very interesting question for future work as we discuss in Section 7.
Remark 4.13. Although Theorem 3.1 is biased in general, the limits in (4.64), (4.65) suggest that this bias
is small for large p and σ large or 0 ≤ρ ≪1 as appropriate for the structure of the target measure and
proposal kernel.
4.3.2

asymptotically comparable to an appropriate over-damped Langevin dynamic as we shall explain next.

bound, (4.93), for the particular case of the Slingshot Algorithm, Theorem 3.4, below in Section 6.

5
Degeneracy in the case of HMC sample path selection
As we said, the methods introduced in the previous two sections, Section 3, Section 4, can be viewed as
multiproposal versions of the RWM algorithm and its many variants and generalizations. On the other hand,
it is also interesting to consider multiproposal implementations of the Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC)
approach to sampling, [DKPR87, Nea11]. Actually there are a number of non-trivial and non-equivalent
ways to introduce multiproposal structure into the HMC paradigm. Here we focus on the case where the
proposal cloud represent the position coordinates of different steps along the (numerical) solution path of
the Hamiltonian dynamic associated to the target measure of interest. This approach has been previously
considered in [Cal14, GHHKM24] and here, unfortunately, our main take away is that such schemes should
be avoided in practice. We mention some other possible approaches to multiproposal HMC sampling not
covered in our analysis herein as an outlook for future work below in Section 7.
5.1
General State Space Formulation
We start by recalling some elements of the HMC approach within the general state space formalism, Section 2,
for our purposes here as follows; see e.g. [Nea11, BRSS18, GHKM23, GHHKM24, GHHK+24] for further
general background. Note that, for simplicity, we restrict the presentation here to the ‘finite-dimensional case’
where our target measure µ has a C1 density supported on Rn. Nevertheless many of the results presented
here and below in Section 5.3 can be easily adapted to other situations; particularly the Hilbert-space (or
preconditioned dimension-free) setting introduced in [BPSSS11] and considered in e.g. [GHKM23, GHM21].
To proceed, we set X = Y = Rd, for some d ∈N, and consider a target measure of the form µ(dq) =

suitable Hamiltonian function H : R2d →R for which the associated Gibbs measure

6
The Slingshot Algorithm
We turn in this final section to some further study of the Slingshot algorithm introduced as Algorithm 1.1,
Theorem 3.4 above. Section 6.1 considers a Gaussian proposal kernel with a state dependent covariance to
optimize the rate of convergence to the large proposal limit in some basic examples. We then take up the
efficacy of the Slingshot against other MCMC algorithms in Section 6.2. Here we provide some promising
initial empirical results, particularly when we employ an adaptive proposal variance. Code and data are
available at https://github.com/andrewjholbrook/infiniteProposals.

6.1
Optimizing Convergence Rates in the Large Proposal Limit
One notable property of this class of methods is that we can introduce a state dependence to the proposal
variance without introducing bias at the p = ∞limit. As we will see, appropriately optimizing over possible
variance structures leads to a faster convergence of the one step proposal kernel to the target measure in
some representative examples.

p, indicating that there may be room for improving the argument to select a more optimal value.

## Page 46


Mad Props: Parallelism in MCMC Through the Lens of the Infinite Proposal Limit
46
multiple draws of p proposals – a metric that, while potentially computationally expensive, is generally
computable across a broad class of proposals. Figure 3 compares the variance of ¯αp across redraws from the
slingshot kernel against the resulting loss, for different numbers of proposals p and different proposal standard
deviations σf. We see that low loss values – that is, strong agreement between the slingshot kernel and the
target distribution – correspond to low variance of ¯αp, i.e., a consistent clustering around its mean value.
This therefore provides a potentially powerful tuning heuristic for tuning σf on general target distributions,
at the expense of having to evaluate ¯αp for multiple sets of proposals. It also provides empirical evidence
that error in the approximation ¯αp ≈Eβ = Z is a key bottleneck for convergence of the Slingshot kernel to
the target distribution as p grows large.
6.2
MCMC Case Studies
We next explore some of the properties of the slingshot sampler Algorithm 1.1 in three separate case studies.
6.2.1
The Effect of the Proposal Kernel on MCMC Convergence
In our first study, we investigate the tuning of the proposal distribution in Algorithm 1.1 to achieve improved
MCMC convergence. In particular, we will increase or decrease the proposal standard deviation adaptively
to target a given acceptance rate, defined for multiproposal MCMC as selecting one of the p new proposals
rather than keeping the current sample for another iteration [Hol23a]. We made these proposal distribution
adjustments by multiplying the precision matrix by the ratio between the target and measured acceptance
rates, clamped to [ 1
2, 2]; these adjustments were made at intervals of exponentially increasing length. As an
example target distribution, we again use the tilted banana distribution given in (6.7). Figure 4 shows the
results of a series of 200,000-sample slingshot MCMC chains for varying numbers of proposals and target
acceptance rates. The left plot shows the proposal standard deviation σ adaptively selected for each target
acceptance rate; smaller, less aggressive values are of course chosen as the target acceptance rate goes up,
while larger p allows more aggressive proposals across a range of target acceptance rates. The middle plot
shows that the target acceptance rates were largely achieved by the given choices of σ; the exceptions were
when p was very small, in which case the Barker acceptance formulation places a limit on how high the
acceptance rate can go.
Finally, the right plot shows the loss by acceptance rate; the loss decreases as
p increases and, for sufficiently large p, the convergence is roughly uniform across a wide range of target
acceptance rates. Figure A1 in the appendix shows a similar set of results for a target distribution made up
of a mixture of Gaussians.
6.2.2
Off-the-Shelf Parallelization
Exploitation of MP-MCMC structure via parallelization is explored in [Hol23b, GHHKM24, LCL+25]. Here,
we focus on easy, off-the-shelf parallelization of the slingshot algorithm with the likes of TensorFlow
[ABC+16].
Figure 5 demonstrates the natural parallelization of the slingshot algorithm and the ability
to efficiently scale the algorithm to massive proposal counts with relatively accessible GPUs. First, with
p = 100,000 Gaussian proposals scaled according to (6.6), we use a single GPU to speed up proposals and
target evaluations, and compare to CPU implementations for multivariate Gaussian targets of increasing
dimensions. We see that our simple GPU implementations are hundreds of times faster than the CPU imple-
mentations, with relative speedups increasing with target dimension. Next, we demonstrate how additional si-
multaneous parallelization across the M components of a uniform mixture distribution
1
M
PM−1
m=0 N2(10m1, I)
facilitates even more proposals when within a fixed timing budget. A GPU-powered chain is able to use
100,000 proposals, while a CPU-powered chain only uses 100. In this experiment, both GPU and CPU
setups adapt proposal standard deviations targeting a 50% acceptance rate. Both take the same amount
of time per iteration but achieve different effective sample sizes (ESS) after 10,000 iterations. We see that
GPU-based parallelization becomes necessary for as few as M = 50 mixture components and barely suffers
for the massively multimodal target with M = 1,000 modes.

6.2.3
Empirical Convergence
While theoretical results indicate that the slingshot algorithm generates samples from the target as p →∞,
Figure 6 describes empirical experiments that demonstrate accuracy for moderate proposal counts (p ≤512),
albeit for relatively simple target distributions: the 2-dimensional normal N2(0, I), the uniform mixture of
2-dimensional normals 1
2N2(0, I)+ 1
2N2(5, I), the 2-dimensional banana distribution (6.7), with a = 1/2, b =
1, c = 0, B = 1, and the 4-dimensional normal distribution N4(0, I). To isolate the effect of proposal count,
we use the same proposal variance regardless of proposal count for each target. All ESSs are more than
1,000 regardless of proposal count, so minimal Monte Carlo error biases results. In general, convergence
as a function of proposal count p appears slowest for the highly-nonlinear banana distribution, followed by
the mixture distribution, the 4-dimensional normal and the 2-dimensional normal, in order. That said, all
distributions demonstrate the same positive trend as proposals increase.
6.2.4
Select Comparisons
Finally, we are interested in the slingshot algorithm’s empirical performance in comparison to a select
collection of non-gradient-informed algorithms, including random walk Metropolis and a number of unbiased
MP-MCMC algorithms. Table 2 describes an experiment that compares these algorithms’ mixing speeds
and estimator accuracies while increasing target dimensionality and keeping slingshot’s proposal count fixed
at 1,000.
Despite this moderate proposal count, the biased slingshot’s 1st and 2nd moment estimators
surprisingly outperform those of its unbiased competitors. That said, increasing target dimensionality with
p fixed leads to a breakdown in estimator accuracy, pointing to the need for a future theoretical investigation
of the d, p →∞joint limit. In addition to providing accurate estimators, the slingshot outperforms its
competitors in terms of mixing speed as measured by both ESS per iteration and ESS per second.

7
Summary and Outlook
The large proposal p-limit and general state space frameworks set out above provide a new and useful lens to
study different forms of non-trivial parallelism within the MP-MCMC paradigm. Following this paradigm we
identify a collection of promising new methods (Algorithm 1.1, Algorithm 3.3 and Algorithm 3.4 and see also
Section 6, Theorem 3.8, Theorem 3.9) while largely ruling out the use of other forms of parallelism that have
been suggested in previous works (Theorem 3.5, Section 5.3, Theorem 5.2). Moreover, in our analysis of the
large p-properties of the one-step Rao-Blackwellization of a class of convolutional methods, (Theorem 4.17) we
encounter some possible disadvantages of existing methods. Finally, our quantitative results (Theorem 4.15)
suggest a means of deriving optimal scaling results as we begin to explore in Section 6.1.
The implications of the results herein and in particular our asymptotic p →∞approach suggest a number
of very interesting and fruitful directions of future research.
1. The P ∞kernel as an intrinsic object to study: The chains derived in Theorem 4.1 and Theorem 4.3 pro-
vide a means of assessing the ultimate limits of multiproposal parallelization for a number of algorithms
considered herein.
In particular, we have in mind the P ∞formulation of [Tje04] and the multipro-
posal pCN methods we recently derived in [GHHKM24, GHHK+24] alongside its Multiple Try variation
given here as Algorithm 3.2, Algorithm 3.3 and Algorithm 3.4. In both cases, the study of mixing rates
for both ∞kernels are of interest in comparative perspective to their single proposal analogues; cf.
[HSV14, GHM21, ALPW24]. Some preliminary steps in this direction have been carried out in soon to
be completed work [CGHMS26].
2. Large p-properties under parameter scaling: The limits established in Theorem 4.1, Theorem 4.3 and
Theorem 4.15 are only a starting point. Here, we note that the general kernels (3.25) and (3.51) are
typically defined up to particular algorithmic parameters, call them ρ, which can be varied with p as
p →∞. In a number of these methods, we may hope to derive an ‘optimal’ choice ρ∗(p).
As a preliminary step in this direction we have carried out some analysis of algorithmic parameter
limits directly at p = ∞in Section 4.3.
For Theorem 3.1, Theorem 3.9, Theorem 3.13 we identify
in Theorem 4.11 some algorithmic parameter limits leading to Monte Carlo sampling directly from the
target measure. This limiting result includes, for example Algorithm 3.3 and its convolutional counterpart
derived in [GHHKM24] with p = ∞and ρ = 0. On the other hand, for Algorithm 3.4, we identify limiting
behavior to a preconditioned overdamped Langevin dynamic derived in [CRSW13] in the ‘small time step
regimen’. See Theorem 4.14 and related earlier results from [GMZ23].
3. Dimensional scaling in the large p-limit: While we have shown previously [GHHKM24, GHHK+24] that
MP-MCMC methods are particularly effective for resolving measures with multimodal or complex geo-
metric structure, it is not clear if the ‘non-preconditioned’ MP-MCMC forms of parallelism originally pro-
posed in [Tje04, LLW00] or our slingshot method (Algorithm 1.1) are more effective for high-dimensional
problems.
We have several approaches in immediate view that can be used to get a better handle on this question.
Regarding the method [Tje04] recalled here as Theorem 3.13, one may aim to carry out a diffusion limit
analysis in the vein of [GGR97, RR98, MPS12, PST12, BPR+13] for the p = ∞processes defined as
special cases of the kernel (4.2) in Theorem 4.1. One interesting question is how the number of samples
required to accurately resolve the target measure under an optimal scaling of algorithmic parameters
in this ∞-limit method compares to benchmarks for RWM, MALA and HMC (O(d), O(d1/3), O(d1/4))
in the aforementioned works. Regarding our slingshot method, we observe above in Section 4.2.3, the
implied p = ∞-limit is simply taking i.i.d. samples from the target measure, an obvious dimension-free
method. However, here it is not clear how fast the convergence as p →∞degenerates as a function of
the dimension.
4. Rigorous bounds and diagnostics for bias: As we lay out above, the most natural MP-MCMC methods
described in Section 3.1 can be corrected by using the Multiple Try approach Section 3.2 or by introducing
a convolutional mechanism Section 3.3. However, as hinted by Theorem 4.17, these unbiased corrections
may have disadvantages over their biased counterpart. Moreover, as we emphasize in Section 4.2.3 the
slingshot algorithm of Theorem 3.4 and Section 6 is unbiased in the p →∞asymptotic.
Note also


## Page 51


Mad Props: Parallelism in MCMC Through the Lens of the Infinite Proposal Limit
51
Theorem 4.12 in regards to Theorem 3.1. Thus, further systematic case studies supplemented by the
development of rigorous frameworks for bias would be desirable to complete the picture and determine
the viability of the Slingshot and other ‘uncorrected’ MP-MCMC methods as general purpose tools.
5. Other forms of parallelism and their large p-limits: The multiproposal formulations we lay out in Sec-
tion 3 are not the only possibilities under the general multiproposal formalism. In particular, while we
largely rule out the use of multiproposal sample-path HMC approaches in Section 5.3, other different
multiproposal HMC formulations remain to be explored. For example, we can consider HMC with mul-
tiple momentum draws at each step and, in this context, consider the associated large p-limit. On the
other hand, we note the new multi-proposal generalization of the elliptic slice sampler, MESS, developed
in [SGHC+26]. While MESS can be viewed as a (degenerate) surrogate HMC trajectory method its
structure is quite different from the asymptotically accurate HMC setup we described in Section 5.3.
6. Rao-Blackwellization and resampling: Other questions remain in regards to the Rao-Blackwellization
(2.10) associated to multiproposal methods.
Here, for example, our analysis in Section 4.5 does not
touch on the Multiple Try or HMC methods. Moreover, one of the aspects of the resampling methods,
briefly mentioned above in Theorem 3.16, is that this approach may give us some of the advantages of
Rao-Blackwellizations while mitigating some of the associated costs in terms of memory storage. Thus, we
may ask how many resampling steps are required to obtain comparable estimator efficiency and ascertain
whether conclusions hold in the methods’ respective large p-limits.

B
Additional Proposal Tuning Experiments
B.1
Convergence of MCMC Results to Target
This section supplements Section 6.2.1 and presents additional numerical experiment related to the tuning of
the proposal distributions to optimize MCMC convergence. Figure A1 shows the results of MCMC sampling
by target acceptance rate for a target distribution consisting of a mixture of three Gaussian distributions,
given by

8. Figure A1 is the analogue of Figure 4, which shows similar results for the tilted banana
distribution given in (6.7).
Each point on the plot represents the results of a chain of 20,000 MCMC
samples. As in Figure 4, the left plot of Figure A1 shows the proposal standard deviation σ for each target
acceptance rate, the middle plots shows that the target acceptance rate was largely achieved by the selected
σ, and the right plot shows loss – defined to be the sum of the relative error in the first, second, and fourth
moments – by target acceptance rate. As in Figure 4, the loss is largely robust across a wide range of target
acceptance rates.

76
Figure A1: Tuning σf for the mixture of Gaussians, by number of proposals p. Each point represents a chain
of 20,000 MCMC samples with σf adaptively tuned to a given target acceptance rate. Left: The choice of σf
by target acceptance rate. Middle: The acceptance rate by target acceptance rate, showing that the target
acceptance rate was achieved up to the limits of the Barker formulation. Right: Loss by acceptance rate,
showing accurate approximation across a broad range of acceptance rates.
